<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that Maker can achieve the Snaky hexomino within 21 actual Maker moves against arbitrary legal Breaker play on the initially empty infinite square board. The same bound holds on a $`17\times17`$ square; in fact, Maker can confine its claims to a fixed 251-cell board.

- **claim-snaky-within-21** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): In this game, there is one globally legal Maker policy that completes an allowed copy of $S$ within at most $21$ actual Maker claims against every legal continuation of Breaker. Equivalently, extend the policy by fresh claims after an earlier win: its Maker set after claim $21$ contains a target whenever the first $20$ Breaker replies are legal.
  - Explanation: A policy fixed before play achieves Snaky from the empty board against every legal Breaker continuation. The bound includes actual replacement claims and permits stopping at an earlier win.
- **claim-full-envelope-composition** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-composition)): Let $(A_i,T_i,h_i)$, $1\le i\le r$, be valid claims, where $1\le r<\infty$, and let $p\in\mathbb Z^2$. Define $$\label{eq:combination} T=\{p\}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^rT_i, \qquad A=\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^r A_i\ \cup\ \bigcap_{i=1}^rT_i\right)\setminus\{p\}, \qquad h=1+\max_i h_i.$$ Then $(A,T,h)$ is valid. Also, at a position with Breaker to move, if $A\cup\{p\}\subseteq M$ and $B\cap T=\varnothing$, then Maker has already won or can win within $h-1$ further Maker moves.
  - Explanation: Combining valid children makes their requirements and common envelope Maker-owned after the pivot or a charged replacement. Every legal Breaker reply therefore leaves a valid child. The additional conclusion starts at Breaker’s turn with the pivot already owned.
- **claim-immediate-completion-bases** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-bases)): Write the points of $S$ in the order in <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a> as $s_0,\ldots,s_5$. The claims $$\label{eq:bases} (A_j,T_j,h_j)=(S\setminus\{s_j\},S,1), \qquad 0\le j<6,$$ are valid.
  - Explanation: Each base requires all but a designated target cell and excludes Breaker from the entire target. Maker either already owns the target or completes it with the missing cell.
- **claim-terminal-certificate** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-certificate)): The data in Appendix <a href="#app:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:data">10</a> have exactly $722$ numbered lines, with indices $6,\ldots,727$. Every line has a nonempty child list, every reference uses an allowed placement of an earlier card, and every parenthesized expression is a finite nonempty combination. Together with the six bases, they produce $728$ valid claims. Their heights are at most $21$, and the last card has $$\label{eq:final-card} p_{727}=(8,8),\qquad A_{727}=\varnothing, \qquad \|T_{727}\|=251,\qquad h_{727}=21.$$ Moreover, $T_{727}\subseteq\{0,\ldots,16\}^2$.
  - Explanation: Backward references and finite combinations reconstruct valid claims. The final required set is empty, and its envelope and height give the stated empty-board and finite-board bounds.
- **claim-finite-board-win** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-finite-board)): Maker wins the weak Maker–Breaker game within $21$ actual Maker claims on the initially empty $251$-cell board $T_{727}$, and also on the initially empty $17\times17$ board $\{0,\ldots,16\}^2$. In each game, Maker moves first, the players alternate claiming one previously unclaimed cell, and the targets are the allowed copies of $S$ contained in the board.
  - Explanation: Envelope nesting keeps prescribed claims inside the final finite envelope. A free replacement remains available throughout the bounded play, so the same move bound holds on either stated finite board.
- **claim-sparse-neighborhood-row-win** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-four-row)): Suppose it is Maker’s turn at an arbitrary finite position, Maker owns the four cells $$\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0)\},$$ and Breaker owns at most three cells in $$Q=([-3,6]\times[-4,4])\cap\mathbb Z^2.$$ Then Maker has already won or can force a win. Additional Maker cells and arbitrary Breaker cells outside $Q$ are allowed.
  - Explanation: The owned row and sparse local Breaker occupancy suffice for a forced win. The proof combines clean endpoint forks, perpendicular attacks, and row extensions while allowing arbitrary ownership outside the specified neighborhood.
- **claim-reduced-envelope-composition** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-r25-composition)): Let $(P_j,E_j,h_j)$, $1\leq j\leq m$, be valid reduced cards already placed in one coordinate plane, where $1\leq m<\infty$. Given an attack cell $x$, put $$\label{r25:reduced-rule} \begin{aligned} U&=\bigcup_jP_j,& V&=\bigcup_jE_j,& I&=\bigcap_jE_j,\\ H&=U\cup I,& P&=H\setminus\{x\},& E&=(V\setminus H)\cup\{x\},\qquad h=1+\max_jh_j. \end{aligned}$$ Then $(P,E,h)$ is valid. Its normalization is exactly the full card obtained by Lemma <a href="#thm:composition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:composition">3</a> from the normalized children $(P_j,P_j\cup E_j,h_j)$ at $x$; in particular, normalization changes neither the required set nor the height.
  - Explanation: Reduced composition preserves the winning meaning without requiring containment or disjointness of the child sets. Normalizing the result agrees exactly with full-envelope composition, including the required set and height.
- **claim-snaky-within-25** (theorem, [source](#stmt-r25-bound)): There is one ordinary Maker policy on the infinite square board that, from the empty position, completes a copy of $S$ within $25$ actual fresh Maker claims against every legal Breaker continuation. In the fixed-endpoint formulation, after extending the policy by fresh moves beyond an earlier win, the Maker set after its $25$th claim contains a target whenever the first $24$ Breaker replies are legal along the policy.
  - Explanation: The terminal reduced card yields a globally legal policy with its own opening and placement convention. Every descent charges an actual fresh claim, and earlier targets persist under the extended policy.
- **claim-conditional-35-interfaces** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-r35-conditional)): The 35-move certificate has 616 cards, including its six base cards, and 1,837 placement terms. Every card satisfies $A_i^{35}\subsetneq T_i^{35}$, $o\in T_i^{35}\setminus A_i^{35}$, and $h_i^{35}\le34$. Four of its conditional interfaces are $$\label{r35:conditional-table} \begin{array}{c\|c\|r\|r\|l} i&A_i^{35}&\|T_i^{35}\|&h_i^{35}&\text{region disjoint from }T_i^{35}\\ \hline 557&\{(-1,0)\}&118&19&\{(x,y):x\le-3,\ y\le-2\}\\ 595&\{(-1,0)\}&222&27&\{(x,y):x=-2,\ y\le-2\}\\ 603&\{(-1,0)\}&275&27&\{(x,y):x\le-3,\ y=0\}\\ 615&\{(1,0)\}&686&34&\{(1,-1)\}. \end{array}$$ Each row applies at any Maker turn with its required point owned and its entire envelope free of Breaker, regardless of all other ownership. For row 615 there is also a Breaker-turn conclusion: if Maker owns $\{o,(1,0)\}$ and Breaker avoids $T_{615}^{35}$, then after any legal next reply Maker wins within 33 further claims, unless already victorious.
  - Explanation: The reconstructed conditional interfaces specify required ownership, complete Breaker-free envelopes, heights, and omitted regions. The final interface also supports a Breaker-turn conclusion when its pivot and requirement are already owned.
- **claim-snaky-within-35** (theorem, [source](#stmt-r35-theorem)): There is one ordinary Maker policy on the initially empty infinite square board that completes an allowed copy of $S$ within 35 actual Maker claims against every legal Breaker play. Equivalently, after extending a won position by fresh moves if necessary, its 35th Maker set contains a target whenever the first 34 Breaker replies are legal. No 35th Breaker reply is required.
  - Explanation: A whole-board opening selects a conditional interface for distant replies and a Breaker-turn interface for nearby replies. Fixed opening and child choices give a single legal policy against every continuation.

```json
        "exact_quote": "<span id=\"lem:combination\" label=\"lem:combination\"></span>Let $(A_i,T_i,h_i)$, $1\\le i\\le r$, be valid claims, where $1\\le r<\\infty$, and let $p\\in\\mathbb Z^2$. Define $$\\label{eq:combination}\n T=\\{p\\}\\cup\\bigcup_{i=1}^rT_i,\n \\qquad\n A=\\left(\\bigcup_{i=1}^r A_i\\ \\cup\\\n             \\bigcap_{i=1}^rT_i\\right)\\setminus\\{p\\},\n \\qquad h=1+\\max_i h_i.$$ Then $(A,T,h)$ is valid.         "anchor": "stmt-r25-composition",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(P_j,E_j,h_j)$, $1\\leq j\\leq m$, be valid reduced cards already placed in one coordinate plane, where $1\\leq m<\\infty$. Given an attack cell $x$, put $$\\label{r25:reduced-rule}\n \\begin{aligned}\n U&=\\bigcup_jP_j,& V&=\\bigcup_jE_j,& I&=\\bigcap_jE_j,\\\\\n H&=U\\cup I,& P&=H\\setminus\\{x\\},&\n E&=(V\\setminus H)\\cup\\{x\\},\\qquad\n h=1+\\max_jh_j.\n \\end{aligned}$$ Then $(P,E,h)$ is valid. Its normalization is exactly the full card obtained by Lemma <a href=\"#thm:composition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:composition\">3</a> from the normalized children $(P_j,P_j\\cup E_j,h_j)$ at $x$; in particular, normalization changes neither the required set nor the height.",
        "source_id": "PM-187.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "exact_quote": "Let $(P_j,E_j,h_j)$, $1\\leq j\\leq m$, be valid reduced cards already placed in one coordinate plane, where $1\\leq m<\\infty$. Its normalization is exactly the full card obtained by Lemma <a href=\"#thm:composition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:composition\">3</a> from the normalized children $(P_j,P_j\\cup E_j,h_j)$ at $x$; in particular, normalization changes neither the required set nor the height.",

        "exact_quote": "The 35-move certificate has 616 cards, including its six base cards, and 1,837 placement terms. Four of its conditional interfaces are $$\\label{r35:conditional-table}\n\\begin{array}{c|c|r|r|l}\n i&A_i^{35}&|T_i^{35}|&h_i^{35}&\\text{region disjoint from }T_i^{35}\\\\ \\hline\n 557&\\{(-1,0)\\}&118&19&\\{(x,y):x\\le-3,\\ y\\le-2\\}\\\\\n 595&\\{(-1,0)\\}&222&27&\\{(x,y):x=-2,\\ y\\le-2\\}\\\\\n 603&\\{(-1,0)\\}&275&27&\\{(x,y):x\\le-3,\\ y=0\\}\\\\\n 615&\\{(1,0)\\}&686&34&\\{(1,-1)\\}.\n\\end{array}$$ Each row applies at any Maker turn with its required point owned and its entire envelope free of Breaker, regardless of all other ownership.         "source_id": "PM-187.1"
      },
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Snaky target** ([source](#eq:target)): Snaky is the six-cell target $$\label{eq:target} S=\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0),(3,1),(4,1)\}\subset\mathbb Z^2.$$
- **Allowed symmetry group** ([source](#eq:target)): Let $\mathcal G$ be the eight $2\times2$ signed permutation matrices: each row and column has one nonzero entry, equal to $1$ or $-1$.
- **Allowed copy** ([source](#eq:target)): An allowed copy is $t+R(S)$ with $t\in\mathbb Z^2$ and $R\in\mathcal G$.
- **Valid claim, required set, envelope, and height** ([source](#stmt-def-claim)): Let $A\subseteq T\subset\mathbb Z^2$ be finite and let $h\ge1$ be an integer. The triple $(A,T,h)$ is a *valid claim* if, at every position with Maker to move and with $$A\subseteq M,\qquad B\cap T=\varnothing,$$ Maker has already completed a target or has a strategy that completes one within $h$ further actual Maker moves. We call $A$ the required set, $T$ the envelope, and $h$ the height.
- **Numbered card** ([source](#sec:certificate)): We use the word *card* for a numbered claim in this $21$-move certificate.
- **Valid reduced card** ([source](#r25:condition)): Let $P,E\subset\mathbb Z^2$ be finite and let $h\geq1$ be an integer. A *valid reduced card* $(P,E,h)$ means the following: at every Maker turn with finite disjoint ownership sets $M,B$, if $$\label{r25:condition} P\subseteq M,\qquad B\cap E=\varnothing,$$ Maker has already won or can win within $h$ further actual Maker claims.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-completion-bases** [claim](#stmt-lem-bases) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-bases): Immediate completion or detection of an already-owned target. -> Valid base claims.
- **step-full-composition** [claim](#stmt-thm-composition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-composition): Claim the pivot or a fresh replacement, then use ownership of the common child envelope to select a surviving continuation. -> A valid parent claim and its Breaker-turn consequence.
- **step-main-certificate-reconstruction** [claim](#stmt-prop-certificate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-certificate): Structural induction on inline expressions followed by induction on backward-referenced numbered cards. -> The asserted terminal empty requirement, envelope, height, and square containment.
- **step-main-policy-extraction** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-certificate-2): Fix the cell enumeration and child order before play, replay the history, and descend through the first surviving child. -> A globally legal policy with the stated bound on actual claims.
- **step-finite-board-restriction** [claim](#stmt-cor-finite-board) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-finite-board): Use nested envelopes to keep prescribed claims inside the terminal envelope and count occupied cells to guarantee finite-board replacements. -> The same claim bound on both stated finite boards.
- **step-clean-five-forks** [claim](#stmt-prop-four-row) / [proof](#pm-h-12): Use a clean endpoint triple to create alternative immediate completions, and use disjoint triples to survive the next reply. -> The local fork tactic used in the row argument.
- **step-perpendicular-endpoint** [claim](#stmt-prop-four-row) / [proof](#pm-h-13): Force endpoint parries and branch on the next reply to obtain a clean fork or an immediate completion. -> A winning endpoint tactic allowing arbitrary ownership elsewhere.
- **step-sparse-row-cases** [claim](#stmt-prop-four-row) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-four-row): Partition by blocked row extensions, then analyze the remaining blocker configurations using reflections and clean endpoint triples. -> The conditional forced win at an arbitrary finite Maker turn.
- **step-reduced-composition-normalization** [claim](#stmt-r25-composition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-r25-composition): Track older Breaker avoidance inside and outside the newly owned set, then prove pointwise agreement with normalized full composition. -> Sound reduced composition with exact normalization and the same height.
- **step-reduced-terminal-reconstruction** [claim](#stmt-r25-bound) / [proof](#r25:reconstruction): Decode backward references and required-cell anchors, reconstruct the reduced sets, and evaluate the terminal union and intersection. -> A valid terminal reduced card with empty requirement and the stated height.
- **step-reduced-policy** [claim](#stmt-r25-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-r25-bound): Fix replacement and child orders, compose affine placements during descent, and extend the policy legally after inconsistent histories or an earlier target. -> A single ordinary policy satisfying the reduced-construction move bound and endpoint formulation.
- **step-conditional-interface-reconstruction** [claim](#stmt-r35-conditional) / [proof](#proof-stmt-r35-conditional): Induct through the separate letter-and-offset recurrence, check finite envelopes against omitted-region predicates, and apply the full composition semantics. -> The conditional interfaces, omitted regions, and final Breaker-turn bound.
- **step-whole-board-35-opening** [claim](#stmt-r35-theorem) / [proof](#proof-stmt-r35-theorem): Classify the first reply by sorted absolute coordinates, place an omitted-region interface for distant replies, and reach the final Breaker-turn interface for nearby replies. -> The ordinary whole-board policy and its precise endpoint guarantee.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-normalized-composition**: Reduced composition and composition of normalized children produce exactly the same required set, full envelope, and height.
- **invariant-surviving-child**: After the parent attack or its charged replacement, every legal new Breaker cell misses a child envelope whose required cells are already owned.
- **invariant-height-accounting**: The parent height includes the actual attack or replacement claim and the largest child height; every child has strictly smaller height.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-required-envelope-containment**: Reject a reduced card whenever its required cells lie outside its reduced envelope. -> This discards permitted reduced representations and valid conditional continuations.
- **anti-free-replacement**: Skip an already-owned attack cell without spending a fresh Maker claim or allowing the ensuing Breaker reply. -> The resulting move count and continuation history no longer match the proved rule.
- **anti-pivot-only-avoidance**: Select a continuation because its next attack cell is free. -> An older Breaker cell can invalidate another cell of the continuation envelope.
- **anti-decoder-interchange**: Interpret reduced placement codes using the orientation convention of another construction. -> The reconstructed placements and resulting conditional sets can change.
- **anti-local-test-global-proof**: Treat agreement of finite set calculations as certification of the complete winning policy. -> Finite reconstruction alone does not supply the semantic induction, opening, or actual move accounting.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The executable construction checks reduced composition against independently reconstructed normalized full composition, including finite reply coverage and height accounting. It covers the finite algebraic core of the reduced-composition proposition. The supplied certificate listings are empty, so it does not reconstruct the terminal certificates or certify the complete empty-board winning policies.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON with pivot, nonempty children, and an optional case label; each child has P, E, h.
# PRECONDITIONS: P and E are finite lists of integer coordinate pairs; h is a positive integer.
# OUTPUTS: Canonically sorted reduced P,E, normalized A,T, and parent h as JSON.
# TIME: Proportional to the supplied finite-set operations and output sorting.
# SPACE: Proportional to the supplied supports and reconstructed parent sets.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact Python integers and finite sets; no floating-point arithmetic.
# FAILURE: Invalid input raises an exception and produces a nonzero exit status.
import json
import sys

def points(value):
    if not isinstance(value, list):
        raise ValueError('coordinate list required')
    out = set()
    for pair in value:
        if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair) != 2 or any(type(v) is not int for v in pair):
            raise ValueError('integer coordinate pair required')
        out.add(tuple(pair))
    return out

def run(data):
    pivot = points([data['pivot']])
    x = next(iter(pivot))
    rows = data['children']
    if not isinstance(rows, list) or not rows:
        raise ValueError('nonempty children required')
    children = []
    for row in rows:
        h = row['h']
        if type(h) is not int or h < 1:
            raise ValueError('positive integer height required')
        children.append((points(row['P']), points(row['E']), h))
    U = set.union(*(P for P,E,h in children))
    V = set.union(*(E for P,E,h in children))
    I = set.intersection(*(E for P,E,h in children))
    H = U | I
    P = H - {x}
    E = (V - H) | {x}
    height = 1 + max(h for P,E,h in children)
    A = P
    T = P | E
    result = {key: [list(p) for p in sorted(value)] for key,value in [('P',P),('E',E),('A',A),('T',T)]}
    result['h'] = height
    return result

if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(json.dumps(run(json.load(sys.stdin)), sort_keys=True, separators=(',', ':')))
```

```json
    "oracle_code": "import json\nimport sys\n\ndef read_points(raw):\n    if type(raw) is not list:\n        raise ValueError('invalid set')\n    result = []\n    for pair in raw:\n        if type(pair) is not list or len(pair) != 2 or any(type(v) is not int for v in pair):\n            raise ValueError('invalid point')\n        point = tuple(pair)\n        if point not in result:\n            result.append(point)\n    return result\n\ndef verify(data, actual, declared):\n    x = read_points([data['pivot']])[0]\n    rows = data['children']\n    if type(rows) is not list or not rows:\n        return False\n    required, reduced, full, heights = [], [], [], []\n    support = [x]\n    for row in rows:\n        p, e, h = read_points(row['P']), read_points(row['E']), row['h']\n        if type(h) is not int or h < 1:\n            return False\n        required.append(p)\n        reduced.append(e)\n        full.append(p + [v for v in e if v not in p])\n        heights.append(h)\n        for v in p + e:\n            if v not in support:\n                support.append(v)\n    # Independent full-card construction: classify each support point by membership.\n    parent_a, parent_t = [], []\n    for v in sorted(support):\n        in_required = any(v in p for p in required)\n        in_every_full = all(v in t for t in full)\n        if v != x and (in_required or in_every_full):\n            parent_a.append(v)\n        if v == x or any(v in t for t in full):\n            parent_t.append(v)\n    parent_e = [v for v in parent_t if v not in parent_a]\n    largest = sorted(heights)[-1]\n    expected = {'P':[list(v) for v in parent_a], 'E':[list(v) for v in parent_e], 'A':[list(v) for v in parent_a], 'T':[list(v) for v in parent_t], 'h':largest + 1}\n    for value in (actual, declared):\n        if type(value) is not dict or set(value) != set(expected):\n            return False\n        if type(value['h']) is not int:\n            return False\n        for key in ('P','E','A','T'):\n            read_points(value[key])\n        if value != expected:\n            return False\n    # Check every potentially legal reply in the parent support.\n    owned = parent_a + [x]\n    if not all(all(v in owned for v in p) for p in required):\n        return False\n    for b in parent_t:\n        if b not in owned and not any(b not in e for e in reduced):\n            return False\n    # Outside the parent support every child envelope is absent by construction.\n    if not all(all(v in parent_t for v in e) for e in reduced):\n        return False\n    if not all(h < expected['h'] for h in heights):\n        return False\n    return True\n\ntry:\n    with open(sys.argv[1], encoding='utf-8') as handle:\n        data = json.load(handle)\n    with open(sys.argv[2], encoding='utf-8') as handle:\n        actual = json.load(handle)\n    declared = json.loads(sys.argv[3])\n    accepted = verify(data, actual, declared)\nexcept Exception:\n    accepted = False\nsys.exit(0 if accepted else 1)\n",
    "output_contract": "Return exactly the fields P, E, A, T, h. Coordinate arrays are lexicographically sorted and contain no duplicates. P,E are the reduced parent; A,T are its normalization; h is the parent height. The independent oracle also checks finite child survival and strict height descent.",
    "reason": "The executable construction checks reduced composition against independently reconstructed normalized full composition, including finite reply coverage and height accounting. It covers the finite algebraic core of the reduced-composition proposition. The supplied certificate listings are empty, so it does not reconstruct the terminal certificates or certify the complete empty-board winning policies.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON with pivot, nonempty children, and an optional case label; each child has P, E, h.\n# PRECONDITIONS: P and E are finite lists of integer coordinate pairs; h is a positive integer.\n# OUTPUTS: Canonically sorted reduced P,E, normalized A,T, and parent h as JSON.\n# TIME: Proportional to the supplied finite-set operations and output sorting.\n# SPACE: Proportional to the supplied supports and reconstructed parent sets.\n# ARITHMETIC: Exact Python integers and finite sets; no floating-point arithmetic.\n# FAILURE: Invalid input raises an exception and produces a nonzero exit status.\nimport json\nimport sys\n\ndef points(value):\n    if not isinstance(value, list):\n        raise ValueError('coordinate list required')\n    out = set()\n    for pair in value:\n        if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair) != 2 or any(type(v) is not int for v in pair):\n            raise ValueError('integer coordinate pair required')\n        out.add(tuple(pair))\n    return out\n\ndef run(data):\n    pivot = points([data['pivot']])\n    x = next(iter(pivot))\n    rows = data['children']\n    if not isinstance(rows, list) or not rows:\n        raise ValueError('nonempty children required')\n    children = []\n    for row in rows:\n        h = row['h']\n        if type(h) is not int or h < 1:\n            raise ValueError('positive integer height required')\n        children.append((points(row['P']), points(row['E']), h))\n    U = set.union(*(P for P,E,h in children))\n    V = set.union(*(E for P,E,h in children))\n    I = set.intersection(*(E for P,E,h in children))\n    H = U | I\n    P = H - {x}\n    E = (V - H) | {x}\n    height = 1 + max(h for P,E,h in children)\n    A = P\n    T = P | E\n    result = {key: [list(p) for p in sorted(value)] for key,value in [('P',P),('E',E),('A',A),('T',T)]}\n    result['h'] = height\n    return result\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    print(json.dumps(run(json.load(sys.stdin)), sort_keys=True, separators=(',', ':')))\n",
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | The reconstruction procedure supports a certificate evaluator that computes conditional winning positions from valid backward references. |
| games_simulation | True | strong | implementable | stated | The paper explicitly extracts a playable Maker policy that selects legal claims and surviving continuations against adversarial Breaker replies. |
| tools | True | strong | implementable | inferred | A verification utility can independently reconstruct the finite cards and check grammar, placements, endpoint identities, and malformed-input rejection. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The composition calculus gives a concrete recursive construction of sufficient winning conditions and a height-decreasing policy for selecting continuations. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | The reduced-card representation removes Breaker-avoidance information already implied by required Maker ownership, while normalization recovers an equivalent full card. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finite integer-set reconstruction supplies no accelerator architecture, memory layout, parallel execution contract, or hardware performance result. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The discussion of checksums concerns literal identity of certificate data and explicitly separates that identity from mathematical correctness; it supplies no cryptographic protocol or security reduction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The policy is fixed by explicit enumeration and child ordering, with no learning rule, training objective, or statistical generalisation guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main theorem provides a sufficient winning bound for its specified game rather than an optimisation procedure over move counts, board sizes, or operational objectives. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The surviving-child argument handles a legal Breaker claim within an alternating ownership game; it does not construct a protocol for concurrent participants, message delivery, or distributed failures. |
| other (Discrete geometry and positional game theory) | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The explicit target coordinates and signed coordinate permutations support exact enumeration and recognition of allowed Snaky placements in finite Maker holdings. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-ordinary-alternating-play** (hypothesis): The winning guarantees require Maker-first ordinary alternating play with permanent ownership and fresh cell claims. Blocked use: ['Apply the surviving-child argument when Breaker claims several cells between Maker turns.', 'Apply the policy to overwritable or disappearing ownership.']
- **limit-weak-game-only** (formal_scope): The results concern weak achievement; they do not establish victory when the opponent also wins by completing its own target. Blocked use: ['Use the Maker policy as a proved winning strategy for the strong achievement game.']
- **limit-rigid-target-copies** (hypothesis): Allowed targets use integral translations and signed coordinate permutations of the stated Snaky shape. Blocked use: ['Reuse the certificates for scaled, sheared, or otherwise deformed targets.']
- **limit-conditional-position-interface** (hypothesis): A full claim applies at Maker’s turn with its required cells owned and its entire envelope free of Breaker. Additional ownership does not remove these obligations. Blocked use: ['Invoke a conditional claim from visible Maker geometry without checking the complete envelope.', 'Use a Maker-turn bound directly at a different starting turn.']
- **limit-nonempty-valid-children** (hypothesis): Semantic composition requires a finite nonempty family of valid child claims. Computing the parent sets alone does not establish child validity. Blocked use: ['Interpret an empty child list using an invented intersection or maximum convention.', 'Promote arbitrary algebraic input triples to proved winning game conditions.']
- **limit-charged-replacement-claims** (edge_case): An already-owned pivot requires a fresh replacement claim when play has not ended. That claim and its ensuing legal Breaker reply remain part of the move accounting. Blocked use: ['Skip an owned pivot without charging a move.', 'Descend to a child without processing the actual reply following a replacement.']
- **limit-complete-envelope-avoidance** (hypothesis): A continuation must preserve avoidance of older Breaker cells as well as the latest reply. A free next pivot alone is insufficient. Blocked use: ['Select a child solely because its pivot is unclaimed.', 'Forget earlier Breaker ownership during affine placement and policy replay.']
- **limit-dialect-specific-reconstruction** (formal_scope): The constructions have separate numbering and placement conventions. Reduced placements use required-cell anchors and their own orientation order. Blocked use: ['Decode reduced placement codes using the main construction’s orientation convention.', 'Choose a reduced placement anchor from its envelope instead of its required set.']
- **limit-reduced-envelope-interpretation** (edge_case): Required cells need not lie in the reduced envelope, and the two sets need not be disjoint. Normalization must retain the required cells in the full envelope. Blocked use: ['Reject a reduced card because its required set lies outside its reduced envelope.', 'Treat the reduced envelope alone as the normalized full envelope.']
- **limit-finite-board-replacements** (hypothesis): The finite-board conclusion uses the stated initially empty boards, envelope nesting, contained targets, and availability of a fresh replacement throughout the bounded play. Blocked use: ['Transfer the same policy to an arbitrary smaller board without verifying containment and replacement availability.', 'Count a target that extends beyond the finite board.']
- **limit-sparse-row-neighborhood** (hypothesis): The local row result requires Maker’s turn, the specified owned row, and at most three Breaker cells in the stated neighborhood. It supplies no explicit move bound of its own. Blocked use: ['Declare every owned four-cell row winning regardless of nearby Breaker occupancy.', 'Assign the empty-board move bound to an arbitrary position satisfying only the row hypothesis.']
- **limit-conditional-interface-opening** (hypothesis): The conditional interfaces require their complete stated ownership and envelope conditions. The ordinary opening separately establishes an applicable interface and accounts for its actual opening claims. Blocked use: ['Apply a conditional height directly from the empty board without establishing its required point.', 'Use the Breaker-turn conclusion without ownership of both the requirement and pivot.']
- **limit-fixed-endpoint-replies** (formal_scope): The fixed-endpoint statements extend an earlier winning policy by fresh moves and assume legality only of the intervening Breaker replies. They require no reply after the winning claim. Blocked use: ['Require an additional Breaker reply after victory.', 'Replace the fixed policy against every continuation with a policy chosen after observing future replies.']
- **limit-upper-bounds-not-optima** (unknown): Optimal move counts and minimal winning board sizes are not established. Blocked use: ['Report the sufficient move bound as an exact minimum.', 'Classify every board smaller than the stated square as losing.']
- **limit-finite-reconstruction-versus-strategy** (formal_scope): Exact finite reconstruction supports the asserted set identities, but the game-theoretic conclusions also require semantic induction, the opening argument, and actual move accounting. Blocked use: ['Treat checksum agreement as mathematical strategy verification.', 'Treat agreement on reconstructed sets as a replacement for the semantic induction.']
- **limit-formal-supplement-scope** (formal_scope): The finite reconstruction supplement concerns the separate conditional-interface certificate. It does not establish a complete winning strategy or the other constructions and finite-board result. Blocked use: ['Describe the finite reconstruction supplement as a formal proof of every winning policy.', 'Infer kernel compilation from finite arithmetic reconstruction.']
- **limit-supplied-literal-data-absence** (unknown): ABSENT: the supplied certificate listings contain no literal rows. The stated terminal identities cannot be independently reconstructed from this supplied corpus alone. Blocked use: ['Claim to have reconstructed every terminal certificate from the supplied listings.', 'Publish a host verification receipt for complete reconstruction without the missing literal data.']
- **limit-panel-algebraic-coverage** (formal_scope): Finite checks of the reduced composition formulas do not establish semantic validity of arbitrary input children or certify a complete winning policy. Blocked use: ['Promote agreement on sample set operations to a universal game-winning certificate.', 'Treat algebraic height descent as an executed history that charges replacements and processes every reply.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 187 (`lean/docs/187.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Snaky in 21 Maker moves](../../preprints/Snaky-in-21-Maker-moves-September-25-2026/article.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> In the Snaky Maker–Breaker game, the players alternately claim cells of $\mathbb Z^2$, and Maker seeks a translated, rotated, or reflected copy of the six-cell Snaky shape. The formalization gives a legal strategy that wins within $21$ actual Maker moves against every legal Breaker play from the empty infinite board. The selected theorem checks disjointness and the move counts throughout play; it does not impose the paper's smaller finite-board restriction.
>
> The linked supplements reconstruct the older $35$-move appendix's finite recursive certificate and prove four conditional winning templates from partial positions, assuming the required Maker cells are present and Breaker avoids the corresponding finite envelope.

Comparator source `SnakyTwentyOne.lean` (2207 bytes, sha256 `6b26e6b265ec08aaf044c3564cb346d6a989cfbf92f0e102af741c6962c988f5`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace SnakyPrototype
variable {α : Type*} [DecidableEq α]
abbrev Cell := ℤ × ℤ
def snaky : Finset Cell := {(0, 0), (1, 0), (2, 0), (3, 0), (3, 1), (4, 1)}
def orient (r : Fin 8) (p : Cell) : Cell :=
  match r.val with
  | 0 => (p.1, p.2)
  | 1 => (p.2, p.1)
  | 2 => (p.1, -p.2)
  | 3 => (-p.2, p.1)
  | 4 => (-p.1, p.2)
  | 5 => (p.2, -p.1)
  | 6 => (-p.1, -p.2)
  | _ => (-p.2, -p.1)
def placement (r : Fin 8) (t p : Cell) : Cell := orient r p + t
def HasSnaky (M : Finset Cell) : Prop :=
  ∃ (r : Fin 8) (t : Cell), snaky.image (placement r t) ⊆ M
end SnakyPrototype
namespace SnakyPrototype.OrdinaryStrategy
variable {α : Type*} [DecidableEq α] [Infinite α]
abbrev Policy (α : Type*) := ℕ → Finset α → Finset α → α
def playState (σ : Policy α) (N : ℕ) (β : ℕ → α) (M₀ B₀ : Finset α) :
    ℕ → Finset α × Finset α
  | 0 => (M₀, B₀)
  | k + 1 =>
      let state := playState σ N β M₀ B₀ k
      (insert (σ (N - k) state.1 state.2) state.1, insert (β k) state.2)
def makerAt (σ : Policy α) (N : ℕ) (β : ℕ → α) (M₀ B₀ : Finset α) (k : ℕ) : α :=
  let state := playState σ N β M₀ B₀ k
  σ (N - k) state.1 state.2
def LegalRepliesBeforeFinal (σ : Policy α) (N : ℕ) (β : ℕ → α)
    (M₀ B₀ : Finset α) : Prop :=
  ∀ k, k + 1 < N →
    β k ∉ insert (makerAt σ N β M₀ B₀ k) (playState σ N β M₀ B₀ k).1 ∧
    β k ∉ (playState σ N β M₀ B₀ k).2
end SnakyPrototype.OrdinaryStrategy

namespace SnakyPrototype
open OrdinaryStrategy

theorem snaky_winning_strategy_21_with_legal_states :
    ∃ σ : Policy Cell,
      (∀ r M B, σ r M B ∉ M ∧ σ r M B ∉ B) ∧
      ∀ β : ℕ → Cell, LegalRepliesBeforeFinal σ 21 β ∅ ∅ →
        HasSnaky (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ 21).1 ∧
        (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ 21).1.card = 21 ∧
        Disjoint (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ 21).1 (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ 20).2 ∧
        ∀ k, k < 21 →
          Disjoint (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ k).1 (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ k).2 ∧
          (playState σ 21 β ∅ ∅ k).2.card = k := by
  sorry

end SnakyPrototype

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that Maker can achieve the Snaky hexomino within 21 actual Maker moves against arbitrary legal Breaker play on the initially empty infinite square board. The same bound holds on a $`17\times17`$ square; in fact, Maker can confine its claims to a fixed 251-cell board.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:target"></a>
Polyomino achievement games ask whether one player can obtain a fixed shape despite an opponent’s attempts to obstruct it. In the weak achievement game, usually called the Maker–Breaker game, only Maker seeks to complete the shape. The opponent need not make a competing copy. Snaky is the six-cell target $$\label{eq:target}
 S=\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0),(3,1),(4,1)\}\subset\mathbb Z^2.$$ Let $\mathcal G$ be the eight $2\times2$ signed permutation matrices: each row and column has one nonzero entry, equal to $1$ or $-1$. An allowed copy is $t+R(S)$ with $t\in\mathbb Z^2$ and $R\in\mathcal G$. Thus reflections and quarter turns are permitted, and scaling is not. Figure <a href="#fig:target" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:target">1</a> shows the target in the coordinates of <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a>.

The board is all of $\mathbb Z^2$, initially empty. Maker moves first, and thereafter Maker and Breaker alternate, each claiming one previously unclaimed cell. Ownership is permanent. Maker wins immediately upon owning every cell of an allowed copy, and may own additional cells. There are no initially owned cells or additional handicap moves. A strategy chooses a legal cell from the complete finite history.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *In this game, there is one globally legal Maker policy that completes an allowed copy of $S$ within at most $21$ actual Maker claims against every legal continuation of Breaker. Equivalently, extend the policy by fresh claims after an earlier win: its Maker set after claim $21$ contains a target whenever the first $20$ Breaker replies are legal.*

</div>

The bound counts Maker’s actual claims, including any replacement moves used in the proof. It corresponds to a win no later than the $41$st individual turn of the alternating game. Corollary <a href="#cor:finite-board" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finite-board">6</a> also gives the same bound on a finite $17\times17$ board. We do not assert optimal move counts or board sizes, or a result for the strong game in which both players seek a copy.

<figure id="fig:target">

<figcaption>The six cells of Snaky. A cell is indexed by the coordinates of its lower left corner. Every integral translation and signed coordinate permutation of this shape is an allowed target.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-target"></a>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Earlier results and methods.

Sieben attributes the introduction of polyomino achievement games to Frank Harary (Sieben 2004, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Sieben2004](#ref-Sieben2004)]. His account records handicap-two strategies of Harary, Harborth and Seemann and the nonexistence of a pairing defense for Snaky, and proves victory in $41$ dimensions (Sieben 2004, sec. 4 and Proposition 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Sieben2004](#ref-Sieben2004)]. Csernenszky, Martin and Pluhár later gave a computer-free proof of the nonexistence of a pairing defense (Csernenszky, Martin, and Pluhár 2011, Theorem 10) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CsernenszkyMartinPluhar2011](#ref-CsernenszkyMartinPluhar2011)]. This rules out a class of Breaker strategies, but does not by itself give Maker a winning strategy.

Halupczok and Schlage-Puchta proved that Snaky wins in three dimensions, loses on an $8\times8$ board, and has planar handicap number at most one (Halupczok and Schlage-Puchta 2007, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[HSP2007](#ref-HSP2007)]. A handicap of one allows Maker two stones on the opening turn, before Breaker’s first reply. Ito and Miyagawa also presented a handicap-one construction (Ito and Miyagawa 2007) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[ItoMiyagawa2007](#ref-ItoMiyagawa2007)]. Sieben subsequently gave a finite proof sequence for that handicap and described the ordinary planar problem as undecided at the time (Sieben 2008, sec. 1 and Appendix B) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Sieben2008](#ref-Sieben2008)].

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> treats the empty planar board without a handicap. We give three complete constructions, with bounds of $21$, $25$, and $35$ Maker moves. They share a composition rule but retain different information and different openings. The $21$-move certificate ends directly with no required Maker stones. The $25$-move construction removes already-owned cells from each Breaker-free region and uses required cells as placement anchors. The $35$-move construction supplies four explicit conditional statements at arbitrary positions and an opening that reaches one of them. We keep these methods explicit because their content is not determined by the empty-board move bounds alone.

Our method belongs to the proof-tree approach to weak achievement studied by Sieben (Sieben 2008, secs. 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Sieben2008](#ref-Sieben2008)]. His finite situations and territory-intersection conditions encode ways of combining continuations at Breaker’s turn. Our conditional positions are stated at Maker’s turn, with a separate Breaker-turn consequence for openings in which the pivot is already owned. The formulation explicitly keeps track of arbitrary previous Maker and Breaker ownership and counts actual future Maker moves. We prove its soundness in Section <a href="#sec:templates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:templates">2</a>. The perpendicular attacks discussed by Halupczok and Schlage-Puchta (Halupczok and Schlage-Puchta 2007, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[HSP2007](#ref-HSP2007)] also illuminate why a line of four Maker stones can exert substantial pressure. Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">5</a> gives a separate, fully stated local result of this kind.

Shaik, Mayer-Eichberger, van de Pol and Saffidine studied bounded-depth QBF encodings of finite Maker–Breaker games, including Snaky on a $5\times5$ board (Shaik et al. 2023, sec. 2.1 and 6) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[ShaikEtAl2023](#ref-ShaikEtAl2023)]. Boucher and Villemaire study QBF encodings for finite strong achievement games on ordinary and toroidal boards (Boucher and Villemaire 2025, secs. 2.2, 3.3–3.4, and 4) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BoucherVillemaire2025](#ref-BoucherVillemaire2025)]. Here the finite object is a proof of sufficient conditions for the infinite weak game. Breaker is free to play outside every displayed region, and the proof covers those replies without a board cutoff.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Proof overview.

A conditional winning position is described by three finite data: a set $A$ that Maker must own, a set $T$ that must contain no Breaker cell, and a bound $h$ on further Maker moves. Starting with the six immediate completions of $S$, we combine such conditions as follows. Maker claims a chosen cell. The required cells of every child condition then belong to Maker; moreover, the intersection of the child envelopes belongs to Maker. Consequently one new Breaker cell cannot lie in every child envelope, and at least one continuation remains available.

Section <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">3</a> specifies $728$ numbered conditions, including the six bases, by exact integer coordinates and backward references. The final one has $A=\varnothing$, an envelope of $251$ cells, and height $21$. Section <a href="#sec:strategy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:strategy">4</a> turns the recursive proof into one policy chosen before Breaker’s play. The independent four-in-a-row result follows in Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">5</a>. Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">6</a> distinguishes the mathematical induction, exact data identities, and independent finite calculations. Appendix <a href="#app:route25" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:route25">7</a> proves the reduced-envelope construction and its five-class opening. Appendix <a href="#app:route35" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:route35">8</a> gives the complete $35$-move construction, its unbounded opening, and its conditional tactics. Appendix <a href="#app:formal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:formal">9</a> describes the finite reconstruction supplement. Appendices <a href="#app:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:data">10</a>–<a href="#app:data35" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:data35">12</a> print the three literal certificates, and Appendix <a href="#app:checker" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:checker">13</a> explains the standalone verification programs.

<a id="sec:templates"></a>
### Conditional winning positions

<span id="sec:calculus" label="sec:calculus"></span>

The purpose of this section is to justify a finite rule that remains valid when Breaker may play anywhere on the infinite board. At a finite position let $M,B\subset\mathbb Z^2$ be the finite disjoint sets currently owned by Maker and Breaker. Their complete contents are retained throughout the argument.

<a id="stmt-def-claim"></a>
<div id="def:claim" class="definition">

**Definition 2**. Let $A\subseteq T\subset\mathbb Z^2$ be finite and let $h\ge1$ be an integer. The triple $(A,T,h)$ is a *valid claim* if, at every position with Maker to move and with $$A\subseteq M,\qquad B\cap T=\varnothing,$$ Maker has already completed a target or has a strategy that completes one within $h$ further actual Maker moves. We call $A$ the required set, $T$ the envelope, and $h$ the height.

</div>

This definition imposes no restriction on additional Maker cells, on Breaker cells outside $T$, or on how the position was reached. In particular, it applies to arbitrary finite disjoint ownership, without requiring a prescribed difference between $|M|$ and $|B|$. Only the starting turn is specified. A *placement* is an affine map $F(x)=Rx+t$ with $R\in\mathcal G$ and $t\in\mathbb Z^2$. A valid claim remains valid under the simultaneous replacement of $A,T$ by $F(A),F(T)$, with the same height: the bijection $F$ transports a legal strategy, preserves disjointness and targets, and does not change the number of moves.

The use of finite sufficient conditions and alternative continuations has a close antecedent in Sieben’s proof trees and situations (Sieben 2008, secs. 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Sieben2008](#ref-Sieben2008)]. His situations consist of a Maker core and a neighborhood without Breaker stones and are stated at Breaker’s turn; the proof trees use an empty intersection of continuation territories. The rule below works with arbitrary finite prior ownership and counts every actual fresh Maker claim. Its quantitative form applies to all three constructions in this article.

<a id="stmt-thm-composition"></a>
<div id="thm:composition" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:combination"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Combination rule). *<span id="lem:combination" label="lem:combination"></span>Let $(A_i,T_i,h_i)$, $1\le i\le r$, be valid claims, where $1\le r<\infty$, and let $p\in\mathbb Z^2$. Define $$\label{eq:combination}
 T=\{p\}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^rT_i,
 \qquad
 A=\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^r A_i\ \cup\
             \bigcap_{i=1}^rT_i\right)\setminus\{p\},
 \qquad h=1+\max_i h_i.$$ Then $(A,T,h)$ is valid. Also, at a position with Breaker to move, if $A\cup\{p\}\subseteq M$ and $B\cap T=\varnothing$, then Maker has already won or can win within $h-1$ further Maker moves.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-composition"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-composition</summary>

<a id="eq:intersection-owned"></a>
*Proof.* Every $A_i$ is contained in $T_i$, so $A\subseteq T$. Suppose first that Maker is to move, $A\subseteq M$, $B\cap T=\varnothing$, and no target is yet complete. Since $p\in T$, Breaker does not own $p$. If $p$ is free, Maker claims it. If $p\in M$, Maker instead claims any free cell. Such a cell exists because the board is infinite and $M\cup B$ is finite. This replacement is one real move. In both cases the resulting Maker set $M'$ contains $$\label{eq:intersection-owned}
 \bigcup_i A_i\ \cup\ \bigcap_iT_i
 \subseteq A\cup\{p\}\subseteq M'.$$ If this move completes a target, stop immediately. Otherwise let $b$ be the next legal Breaker cell. As $b\notin M'$, <a href="#eq:intersection-owned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intersection-owned">[eq:intersection-owned]</a> implies $b\notin\bigcap_iT_i$. Choose an index $i$ with $b\notin T_i$. The older Breaker cells also avoid $T_i$, since $T_i\subseteq T$. Thus, at the next Maker turn, $$A_i\subseteq M',\qquad (B\cup\{b\})\cap T_i=\varnothing.$$ The child claim gives a win within $h_i$ more Maker moves. Including the move already spent gives at most $1+h_i\le h$.

For the Breaker-turn assertion, the inclusions in <a href="#eq:intersection-owned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intersection-owned">[eq:intersection-owned]</a> already hold with $M$ in place of $M'$. After Breaker’s next move the same surviving child applies, without spending the parent Maker move. Its bound is at most $\max_i h_i=h-1$. ◻

</details>

The replacement move cannot invalidate this argument: it only adds Maker ownership, and its actual ensuing Breaker reply is included in the proof. It must not be omitted from the count. A reply outside $T$ misses every child envelope, so distant play requires no separate case analysis. The Breaker-turn assertion also shows that $p$ need not have been Maker’s most recent claim.

<a id="stmt-lem-bases"></a>
<div id="lem:bases" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:bases"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Six bases). *Write the points of $S$ in the order in <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a> as $s_0,\ldots,s_5$. The claims $$\label{eq:bases}
 (A_j,T_j,h_j)=(S\setminus\{s_j\},S,1),
 \qquad 0\le j<6,$$ are valid.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-bases"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-bases</summary>

*Proof.* If Maker owns $s_j$, it already owns all of $S$. Otherwise $s_j$ is free because Breaker owns none of $S$. Claiming it completes the target in one move. ◻

</details>

Every application of Lemma <a href="#lem:combination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:combination">[lem:combination]</a> may use placed versions of earlier claims. We will describe a finite collection of such applications and calculate its last required set and height. The semantic proof is already complete: once that required set is empty, the final height bounds play from the empty board.

<a id="sec:certificate"></a>
### The certificate and its final condition

This section specifies the finite applications of the combination rule. We use the word *card* for a numbered claim in this $21$-move certificate. Its numbering is independent of the $25$- and $35$-move tables in Appendices <a href="#app:route25" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:route25">7</a> and <a href="#app:route35" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:route35">8</a>. Numbered references allow a previously proved claim to be reused; an expression written in parentheses is simply an additional, unnumbered application of the same rule. The complete literal data are in Appendix <a href="#app:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:data">10</a>.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Coordinates, placements, and expressions

The coordinate alphabet is

<div class="center">

`0123456789ABCDEFG`.

</div>

A two-character word `XY` denotes the point $(x,y)$ whose coordinates are the positions of `X` and `Y` in this alphabet, counting from zero. For example, `88` means $(8,8)$, `7A` means $(7,10)$, and `GG` means $(16,16)$. The encoded pivots and translation vectors have nonnegative coordinates; the sets produced after signed transformations may contain negative coordinates.

<a id="eq:symmetries"></a>
A reference `j:sUV` means: take numbered card $j$, apply the signed coordinate permutation $R_s$, and then translate by the point encoded by `UV`. To compute $R_s$, negate the first coordinate if the bit of weight $2$ is set, negate the second if the bit of weight $4$ is set, and *then* interchange the coordinates if the bit of weight $1$ is set. The resulting maps are $$\label{eq:symmetries}
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
 s&0&1&2&3\\ \hline
 R_s(x,y)&(x,y)&(y,x)&(-x,y)&(y,-x)\\[2pt]
\end{array}
\quad
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
 s&4&5&6&7\\ \hline
 R_s(x,y)&(x,-y)&(-y,x)&(-x,-y)&(-y,-x).
\end{array}$$ These are precisely the eight matrices in $\mathcal G$. A bare reference `j` uses the identity placement. References change neither the child’s height nor the meaning of its winning condition.

Every data line consists of its decimal card number $j$, a pivot coordinate, and a nonempty ordered list of children. Each child is either a reference or an expression of the form

<div class="center">

`(XY child child ...)`.

</div>

Such an expression combines its children at the pivot `XY` using <a href="#eq:combination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combination">[eq:combination]</a>. All coordinates inside parentheses remain in the coordinate frame of the line. They are not offsets from the enclosing pivot. When the entire card is later placed by an affine map, that map transports every pivot and set in its proof.

Cards $0,\ldots,5$ are the six bases <a href="#eq:bases" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bases">[eq:bases]</a>. The data specify cards $6,\ldots,727$ in order. Every numbered reference in a line, including a reference inside parentheses, is to a smaller card number. We evaluate innermost expressions first, using <a href="#eq:combination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combination">[eq:combination]</a> for the sets and height. Consequently structural induction on each expression, followed by induction on the line number, proves that every resulting card is a valid claim. The finite calculation determines which sufficient conditions this particular list produces.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Two elementary cards

The first two lines illustrate both the geometry and the encoding:

```
6 14 4:100 5:101
7 05 6:001 6:405
```

For card $6$, the two bases are first transposed; the second is also translated by $(0,1)$. Direct substitution gives <a id="eq:first-card"></a>$$\label{eq:first-card}
 A_6=\{(0,y):0\le y\le4\},\qquad
 T_6=A_6\cup\{(1,3),(1,4),(1,5)\},\qquad h_6=2.$$ Claiming $(1,4)$ prepares two copies, completed respectively at $(1,3)$ and $(1,5)$. Unless a copy is already complete, Breaker can occupy at most one of these cells. Figure <a href="#fig:fork" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:fork">2</a> shows the two alternatives.

<figure id="fig:fork">

<figcaption>Card $6$: dots are required Maker cells, the square is the next pivot, and circles mark the two possible completions. The vertical guides indicate the two four-cell bodies. The envelope contains no Breaker cell; any of its additional cells may already belong to Maker.</figcaption>
</figure>

In the right column (x = 1), there are three solid black dots, an open circle labeled "completion (1, 3)", an open square labeled "pivot (1, 4)", and an open circle labeled "completion (1, 5)".


For card $7$, the two copies of $A_6$ both occupy the column $x=0$, $1\le y\le5$. Their additional envelope cells have heights $4,5,6$ and $0,1,2$, respectively. These triples are disjoint, so the intersection of the envelopes is precisely the common column. Deleting the pivot $(0,5)$ therefore gives $$\begin{split}
 A_7&=\{(0,y):1\le y\le4\},\qquad h_7=3,\\
 T_7&=\{(0,y):1\le y\le5\}
       \cup\{(1,y):y\in\{0,1,2,4,5,6\}\}.
 \end{split}$$ The general certificate repeats this mechanism with longer conditional continuations and cells that need not lie near the current line of Maker stones.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The final card

<a id="stmt-prop-certificate"></a>
<div id="prop:certificate" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:final-card"></a>
**Proposition 5**. *The data in Appendix <a href="#app:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:data">10</a> have exactly $722$ numbered lines, with indices $6,\ldots,727$. Every line has a nonempty child list, every reference uses an allowed placement of an earlier card, and every parenthesized expression is a finite nonempty combination. Together with the six bases, they produce $728$ valid claims. Their heights are at most $21$, and the last card has $$\label{eq:final-card}
 p_{727}=(8,8),\qquad A_{727}=\varnothing,
 \qquad |T_{727}|=251,\qquad h_{727}=21.$$ Moreover, $T_{727}\subseteq\{0,\ldots,16\}^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-certificate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-certificate</summary>

*Proof.* The syntactic and set assertions are a finite calculation from <a href="#eq:bases" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bases">[eq:bases]</a>, <a href="#eq:combination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combination">[eq:combination]</a>, and <a href="#eq:symmetries" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:symmetries">[eq:symmetries]</a>, using the full displayed data. The verifier in Appendix <a href="#app:checker" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:checker">13</a> reconstructs every numbered card. Its arrays initially contain the six bases. Before line $j$ is processed, their entries at indices below $j$ are exactly the sets and heights specified by the preceding lines. A reference retrieves an earlier entry and applies its stated placement. Recursive evaluation applies <a href="#eq:combination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combination">[eq:combination]</a> at every parenthesized node and at the root of the line. The nonempty-child and backward-reference tests make every union, intersection, maximum, and lookup well-defined. This proves the invariant for the next line and identifies the computed sets with the mathematically defined ones. The final tests give <a href="#eq:final-card" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-card">[eq:final-card]</a>, maximum height $21$, and the intermediate values in Table <a href="#tab:top" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:top">1</a>. The separate reconstruction `verification/supporting/independent_evaluator.py` also computes all sets and heights and checks the successive intersections. Validity then follows from Lemmas <a href="#lem:combination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:combination">[lem:combination]</a> and <a href="#lem:bases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bases">4</a>, by the structural induction just described. ◻

</details>

For clarity, Table <a href="#tab:top" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:top">1</a> records how the final empty requirement arises. The last line has $32$ placed children, grouped by their six card numbers. Each placed required set is exactly $\{(8,8)\}$. The last column is the number of cells other than $(8,8)$ in the intersection of the envelopes from that group and all preceding groups. The full placements occur in the last line of the certificate.

<div id="tab:top">

| Card $j$ |       $A_j$ | $|T_j|$ | $h_j$ | Placements | Remaining cells |
|---------:|------------:|--------:|------:|-----------:|----------------:|
|      648 | $\{(4,5)\}$ |      87 |    15 |          4 |              35 |
|      708 | $\{(8,8)\}$ |     225 |    20 |          4 |              31 |
|      712 | $\{(5,5)\}$ |      96 |    15 |          8 |              21 |
|      713 | $\{(5,5)\}$ |      98 |    15 |          4 |              12 |
|      725 | $\{(7,7)\}$ |     167 |    20 |          8 |               4 |
|      726 | $\{(7,7)\}$ |     169 |    20 |          4 |               0 |

The six groups of children in card $727$. All their requirements become the pivot. Their common envelope has no other cell, so the combination rule leaves an empty required set.

</div>

In particular, the intersection of all child envelopes is exactly $\{(8,8)\}$: it contains that point because every child requirement does, and the last table entry removes every other point. The union of the child requirements is the same singleton. Equation <a href="#eq:combination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:combination">[eq:combination]</a> therefore gives $A_{727}=\varnothing$. The maximum child height is $20$, giving the bound $21$.

The data also illustrate how play may leave an immediately adjacent line. After Maker plays $(8,8)$ and Breaker replies $(7,8)$, the placed child `708:100` is available. Its pivot is $(8,7)$. If Breaker next takes $(8,6)$, the child `684:011` of card $708$, still under the outer transposition, is available and has pivot $(11,8)$. These are legal illustrative choices, not imposed responses by Breaker. Every other reply is covered by the same intersection rule.

<a id="sec:strategy"></a>
### One strategy against every continuation

We now turn the final card into a single strategy. Making its choices definite also clarifies that the height counts actual Maker moves.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-certificate-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-certificate</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix an enumeration of $\mathbb Z^2$: order cells by increasing $\max(|x|,|y|)$, then lexicographically within each finite level. Retain the printed order of the children in every combination expression. These choices are fixed before any play. Initially the active claim is card $727$ in its identity placement. Its hypotheses hold on the empty board by <a href="#eq:final-card" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-card">[eq:final-card]</a>.

At an active combination with current placement $F$, claim its placed pivot $F(p)$ if free. If Maker already owns $F(p)$, claim the first free cell in the fixed enumeration instead. The invariant of Definition <a href="#def:claim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:claim">2</a> guarantees that Breaker does not own the pivot, and finiteness of ownership guarantees a free replacement. Stop if a target is complete. Otherwise, after Breaker’s next legal reply, choose the first child in the printed list whose placed envelope avoids that reply. Lemma <a href="#lem:combination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:combination">[lem:combination]</a> proves that such a child exists and that all its requirements and its full envelope satisfy the invariant, including the older Breaker cells. For a referenced card whose reference placement is $G$, the new placement is $F\circ G$; for an inline expression, retain $F$ and use that expression.

At a placed base card, either the target is already present or its one missing cell is free. In the latter case claim it and win. Thus a nonterminal step spends one actual Maker move and passes, after one actual Breaker reply, to a child of strictly smaller height. The bases have height $1$. Starting at height $21$, this procedure wins within $21$ Maker moves.

The active expression and placement are determined by the complete history: replay the prescribed pivot or replacement choices and the first-surviving-child choices from card $727$. On a history inconsistent with an earlier prescribed Maker choice, use the first free cell of the enumeration. Use the same fallback on an already-won history if one chooses to continue it formally. This defines a globally legal policy on all finite legal histories, including histories not reached from the empty board under the policy. No choice of the policy depends on future Breaker moves. The preceding induction applies to every legal continuation from the empty board and proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

Only replies before victory are used in this argument. If the first win occurs on Maker move $m\le21$, there have been precisely $m-1$ Breaker moves. In particular, no reply after Maker’s winning move is assumed or required.

One can express the same quantifier order using a fixed endpoint. Extend the policy beyond a completed target by the first-free-cell rule. For every sequence of proposed Breaker cells whose first $20$ entries are legal along this extended policy, the Maker set immediately after move $21$ contains an allowed copy of $S$. An earlier completed copy persists because ownership is permanent. This formulation is $\exists\sigma\,\forall\beta$, with $\sigma$ chosen in the proof and $\beta$ the sequence of Breaker replies; it makes no assumption about a twenty-first Breaker reply. Stopping at the first target recovers the ordinary game.

<a id="stmt-cor-finite-board"></a>
<div id="cor:finite-board" class="corollary">

**Corollary 6** (A finite board). *Maker wins the weak Maker–Breaker game within $21$ actual Maker claims on the initially empty $251$-cell board $T_{727}$, and also on the initially empty $17\times17$ board $\{0,\ldots,16\}^2$. In each game, Maker moves first, the players alternate claiming one previously unclaimed cell, and the targets are the allowed copies of $S$ contained in the board.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-finite-board"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-finite-board</summary>

*Proof.* Evaluation of the literal certificate gives $$T_{727}\subseteq\{0,\ldots,16\}^2,$$ in addition to the endpoint data in <a href="#eq:final-card" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-card">[eq:final-card]</a>. Every placed child envelope is contained in its parent envelope, and every pivot and base target lies in its own envelope. Thus all prescribed pivots and base completions lie in $T_{727}$. Use that strategy, replacing an already-owned pivot by the first free cell in a fixed ordering of $T_{727}$. Before Maker claim $m\le21$, at most $2(m-1)\le40$ cells have been occupied, so such a replacement is always available on either board.

After a pivot or replacement claim, Maker owns every child requirement and the intersection of the child envelopes. Every legal Breaker reply therefore leaves a surviving child envelope; nesting also keeps the older Breaker cells outside that envelope. This includes replies outside $T_{727}$ on the square board. The same height induction now gives victory within $21$ actual Maker claims, counting each replacement. Play stops as soon as a target is complete; no Breaker reply after victory is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### Four stones in a row

The certificate establishes the empty-board theorem without assuming that nearby Breaker cells stay sparse. A separate local statement explains some of the geometry behind its threats. The perpendicular attack below is related to the four-in-a-row tactics of Halupczok and Schlage-Puchta (Halupczok and Schlage-Puchta 2007, sec. 3, pp. 13–15) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[HSP2007](#ref-HSP2007)]; their planar row extensions also appear in Sections 4.4–4.6 of that paper. We give a complete proof for the precise finite-neighborhood hypothesis used here.

<a id="stmt-prop-four-row"></a>
<div id="prop:four-row" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7**. *Suppose it is Maker’s turn at an arbitrary finite position, Maker owns the four cells $$\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0)\},$$ and Breaker owns at most three cells in $$Q=([-3,6]\times[-4,4])\cap\mathbb Z^2.$$ Then Maker has already won or can force a win. Additional Maker cells and arbitrary Breaker cells outside $Q$ are allowed.*

</div>

Call a set *clean* if it contains no Breaker cell. Throughout this section, an instruction to claim an already-owned Maker cell means to claim any free cell instead, unless a target has already been completed. Such a replacement is a real move and allows exactly one new Breaker reply. Extra Maker cells therefore cause no difficulty in any of the arguments below.

When a move is said to force Breaker to a cell $q$, the precise meaning is that $q$ completes an allowed target on Maker’s next move. If $q$ is already Maker’s, the target is already complete. Otherwise $q$ is free, and Breaker must claim it or allow that immediate completion. Every forced continuation is considered only when Maker has not already won or obtained this next-move win.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### A five-cell fork

<a id="eq:horizontal-triple"></a>
Suppose Maker owns the horizontal five $\{(r,0),\ldots,(r+4,0)\}$. For an endpoint $e\in\{r,r+4\}$ and a sign $\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}$, put $$\label{eq:horizontal-triple}
 H(e,\varepsilon)=
 \{(e-1,\varepsilon),(e,\varepsilon),(e+1,\varepsilon)\}.$$ If this triple is clean at Maker’s turn, claiming its middle cell $(e,\varepsilon)$ creates two one-cell completions, at its other two cells. The two targets use respectively the first and last four-cell subrows of the five; they are reflected or translated copies of $S$. Breaker can occupy at most one completion, so Maker wins on the next move unless it has already won. This is the horizontal version of Figure <a href="#fig:fork" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:fork">2</a>.

The four triples in <a href="#eq:horizontal-triple" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-triple">[eq:horizontal-triple]</a> are pairwise disjoint. Consequently, if Maker has just created the five and at least two of these triples are clean *before* Breaker’s response, at least one remains clean afterwards, and the fork wins. The same assertion holds after interchanging the coordinates. For a vertical five we use the notation $$V(c,d)=\{(c,d-1),(c,d),(c,d+1)\},$$ where $c\in\{-1,1\}$ and $d$ is an endpoint height.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The perpendicular endpoint attack

We first establish a local tactic at the right endpoint of a row. Assume Maker owns the four cells $$(-3,0),\quad(-2,0),\quad(-1,0),\quad(0,0),$$ and the set <a id="eq:endpoint-zone"></a>$$\label{eq:endpoint-zone}
 Z=\{(x,y):x\in\{-1,0,1\},\ 1\le |y|\le4\}$$ is clean. Other ownership is arbitrary. Maker claims $(0,1)$, forcing $(1,1)$, and then $(0,-1)$, forcing $(1,-1)$. Indeed, the original horizontal body with the respective pair of cells above or below its endpoint is an allowed Snaky. If Breaker declines either forced reply, Maker completes that copy on its next turn.

After those two replies, Maker claims $(0,2)$. Denote the new Breaker reply by $b$, and set $$E=\{(0,-2)\}\cup V(-1,-2)\cup V(-1,2).$$ Figure <a href="#fig:endpoint-branches" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:endpoint-branches">3</a> separates the two possible continuations after this reply. If $b\notin E$, Maker claims $(0,-2)$. It now owns the vertical five from height $-2$ to height $2$, and both $V(-1,-2)$ and $V(-1,2)$ are clean before the ensuing Breaker reply. They are disjoint, and neither of the two earlier forced Breaker cells is in either triple. The five-cell fork therefore wins.

If $b\in E$, Maker instead claims $(0,3)$, creating the vertical five from height $-1$ to height $3$. The triple $V(1,3)$ is clean. The triple $V(-1,3)$ is also clean unless $$b=(-1,2)\quad\hbox{or}\quad b=(-1,3).$$ If both are clean, the same two-triple argument wins. In either exceptional case there is instead an immediate completion at $q=(-1,-1)$, because Maker already owns the other five cells of <a id="eq:perpendicular-copy"></a>$$\label{eq:perpendicular-copy}
 \{(0,3),(0,2),(0,1),(0,0),(-1,0),(-1,-1)\}
 =\{(-y,3-x):(x,y)\in S\}.$$ The cell $q$ lies in the initially clean zone, and differs from $b$ and from the two earlier forced replies. It is thus free unless Maker already owns it, in which case the target is complete. It also lies outside $V(1,3)=\{(1,2),(1,3),(1,4)\}$. Breaker must either claim $q$, leaving that clean triple for the five-cell fork, or leave $q$ as an immediate winning move. This completes the endpoint tactic.

<figure id="fig:endpoint-branches">

<figcaption>Alternative endpoint continuations after the reply $b$, which is not drawn; here $o = (0,0)$. Dots show guaranteed Maker cells, crosses the two earlier forced Breaker cells, and each square the prescribed next claim. Solid outlines enclose clean triples, whose cells may already be Maker-owned. On the left, their union with the square is $E$. On the right, the dashed triple is clean except when $b = (−1,2)$ or $b = (−1,3)$; in those cases, claiming $(0,3)$ leaves the diamond $q$ as an immediate completion while $V(1,3)$ stays clean. The panels are conditional alternatives, not consecutive moves.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-endpoint-branches"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:endpoint-branches`)
